b_{k,1},b_{k,2},...,b_{k,n}\in\mathrm{Her}(a)_{+}^{\bf 1}
such that

[b_{k,i}]=[b_{k,1}],

i=1,2,...,n,

\displaystyle d_{\tau}(f_{1/k}(a^{1/m_{k}})){{<}}nd_{\tau}(f_{1/k}(b_{k,1}))+\varepsilon\,\,\,\text{for\,\,\,all}\,\,\,\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w}

(e 8.20)

and, an integer
l(k)\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\displaystyle nd_{\tau}(f_{1/k}(b_{k,1}))\leq d_{\tau}(f_{1/l(k)}(a))\,\,\,\text{for\,\,\,all}\,\,\,\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w}.

(e 8.21)

Proof.

Fix
n\in\mathbb{N}.

Since
A
has property (TM), for each
m\in\mathbb{N},
there exists a c.p.c. order zero map

\varphi_{m}:M_{n}\to{\rm Her}(a)
such that

\displaystyle\|a^{1/m}-a^{1/m}\varphi_{m}(1_{n})\|_{{}_{2,\overline{QT(A)}^{w}}}<1/2^{m}.

(e 8.22)

Define
c:=\Pi(\{a^{1/m}\}{{{}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}}})
and
\varphi:M_{n}\to l^{\infty}(A)/I_{{}_{\overline{QT(A)}^{w}}}

such that
\varphi(f)=\Pi(\{\varphi_{m}(f)\})
for all
f\in M_{n}.

Then

\displaystyle c=c\varphi(1_{n})=\varphi(1_{n})c=c.

(e 8.23)

It follows that
c=cf_{1/2}(\varphi(1_{n}))=f_{1/2}(\varphi(1_{n}))c=c.

Thus
c\leq f_{1/2}(\varphi(1_{n})).

Let
\varepsilon>0.

By (1) of Lemma
7.2
, for each integer
k\geq 2,
there exists
m_{k}{{\geq r}}\in\mathbb{N}
such that, for all
m\geq m_{k},

\displaystyle[f_{1/k}(a^{1/m})]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle[f_{1/2}(\varphi_{m}(1_{n}))]+[d_{m}]

(e 8.24)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle[f_{1/k}(\varphi_{m}(e_{1,1}))+f_{1/k}(\varphi_{m}(e_{2,2}))+\cdots f_{1/k}(\varphi_{m}(e_{n,n}))]+[d_{m}],

(e 8.25)

where
\sup\{d_{\tau}(d_{m}):\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w}\}<\varepsilon/2.

Put
b_{k,i}=\varphi_{m_{k}}(e_{i,i})

1\leq i\leq n
and
k\in\mathbb{N}.

Then (
e 8.20
) holds.
On the other hand, since
\varphi_{m_{k}}(1_{n})\in\mathrm{Her}(a),

for each
k,
there is
l(k)\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\displaystyle\|f_{1/l(k)}(a)\varphi_{m_{k}}(1_{n})f_{1/l(k)}(a)-{{\varphi_{m_{k}}}}(1_{n})\|<1/4k.

(e 8.26)

It follows that (see Proposition 2.2 of
[
43
]
), for
k\geq 2,

\displaystyle f_{1/k}(\varphi_{{m_{k}}}(1_{n}))\lesssim f_{{1/l(k)}}(a)\,\,\,{\rm and}\,\,\,n[f_{1/k}(b_{k,1})]=[f_{1/k}(\varphi_{{m_{k}}}(1_{n}))].

(e 8.27)

Then (
e 8.21
) holds.
∎

Theorem 8.7
.

Let
A
be a
\sigma
-unital simple
C^{*}
-algebra
with
QT(A)\not=\{0\}.

If
A
has strict comparison and property (TM),
then
\Gamma
is surjective (see
2.13
).

Proof.

It suffices to prove the proposition for the case that
A={\rm Ped}(A).

We claim that, for any
a\in{\rm Ped}(A\otimes{\cal K})_{+}^{\bf 1}\setminus\{0\}
and any integer
n\in\mathbb{N},

there exists
b\in(A\otimes{\cal K})_{+}

such that

\displaystyle n\widehat{[b]}\leq\widehat{[a]}\leq(n+1)\widehat{[b]}.

(e 8.28)

Case (1):
0
is an isolated point of
{\rm sp}(a).

In this case we may assume that
a=p
for some projection
p.

Choose

\displaystyle\eta:=({1\over{(n+1)^{2}}})\inf\{\tau(p):\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w}\}>0.

(e 8.29)

Note that
f_{1/k}(p)=p
for all
k>1.

Applying Lemma
8.6
, we obtain
b\in\mathrm{Her}(a)_{+}
such that

\displaystyle nd_{\tau}(b)\leq d_{\tau}(p)<nd_{\tau}(b)+\eta\,\,\,{\rm for\,\,\,all}\,\,\,\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w}.

(e 8.30)

Then we compute that

\displaystyle n\widehat{[b]}\leq\widehat{[a]}\leq(n+1)\widehat{[b]}.

(e 8.31)

Case (2):
0
is not an isolated point of
{\rm sp}(a).

We will use Lemma
8.6
for an induction argument.
Put

\displaystyle\sigma_{0}:=\inf\{\tau(a):\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w}\}>0.

(e 8.32)

Fix
n\in N.

Since
0
is a not an isolated point of
{\rm sp}(a),

for each integer
k,
there is a smallest integer
{{J}}(k)>k

such that

\displaystyle f_{1/J(k)}(a)-f_{1/k}(a)\not=0.

(e 8.33)

Define, for each
k,

\displaystyle\sigma_{k}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\inf\{d_{\tau}(f_{1/J(k)}(a))-d_{\tau}(f_{1/k}(a)):\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w}\}>0\,\,\,{\rm and}\,\,\,

(e 8.34)

\displaystyle\eta_{k}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\min\{\sigma_{j}:{{0}}\leq j\leq k+1\}/2^{k+1}(n+1).

(e 8.35)

Applying Lemma
8.6
,
there are mutually orthogonal elements
b_{1,1},b_{1,2},...,b_{1,n}\in\mathrm{Her}(a)_{+}^{\bf 1}
such that

[b_{1,i}]=[b_{1,1}],

i=1,2,...,n,
and, for some
m_{1}\in\mathbb{N},

\displaystyle{{d_{\tau}}}(f_{1/2}(a^{1/m_{1}})){{<}}nd_{\tau}(f_{1/2}(b_{1,1}))+\eta_{1}\,\,\,\text{for\,\,\,all}\,\,\,\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w},

(e 8.36)

and, an integer
l(1)\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\displaystyle nd_{\tau}(f_{1/2}(b_{1,1}))\leq{{d_{\tau}}}(f_{1/l(1)}(a))\,\,\,\text{for\,\,\,all}\,\,\,\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w}.

(e 8.37)

Put
c_{1}:=f_{1/2}(b_{1,1}).

Then, for all
\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w},

\displaystyle\widehat{[f_{1/2}(a^{1/m_{1}})]}(\tau)\leq n\widehat{[c_{1}]}(\tau)+\eta_{1}\,\,\,{\rm and}\,\,\,n\widehat{[c_{1}]}(\tau)\leq\widehat{[f_{1/l(1)}(a)]}(\tau).

(e 8.38)

Choose
k_{2}>l(1)
such that
k_{2}\geq J(l(1)).
Applying Lemma
8.6
,
we obtain
m_{2}\geq m_{1}
and mutually orthogonal
b_{2,1},b_{2,2},...,b_{2,n}\in\mathrm{Her}(a)_{+}^{\bf 1}

with
b_{2,j}\sim b_{2,1}
(
1\leq j\leq n
) and
l(k_{2})\in\mathbb{N}

such that

\displaystyle d_{\tau}(f_{1/k_{2}}(a^{1/m_{2}}){{)}}<nd_{\tau}(f_{1/k_{2}}(b_{2,1}))+\eta_{l(1)}\,\,\,{\rm and}\,\,\,

(e 8.39)

\displaystyle nd_{\tau}(b_{2,1})\leq d_{\tau}(f_{1/l(2)}(a))\,\,\,{\rm for\,\,\,all}\,\,\,\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w}.

(e 8.40)

Put
c_{2}=f_{1/k_{2}}(b_{2,1}).
Then, for all
\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w},

\displaystyle[f_{1/k_{2}}(a^{1/m_{2}}){{\widehat{]}}}(\tau)\leq n\widehat{[c_{2}]}(\tau)+\eta_{l(1)}\,\,\,{\rm and}\,\,\,n\widehat{[c_{2}]}(\tau)\leq[f_{1/l(2)}(a){{\widehat{]}}}(\tau).

(e 8.41)

We compute that, for all
\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w},
by (
e 8.39
) and (
e 8.37
)),
(
e 8.34
)
(recall that
k_{2}>J(l(1))
),

\displaystyle nd_{\tau}(f_{1/k_{2}}(b_{2,1}))

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle d_{\tau}(f_{1/k_{2}}(a^{1/m_{2}}))-\eta_{l(1)}

(e 8.42)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d_{\tau}(f_{1/l(1)}(a))+(d_{\tau}(f_{1/k_{2}}(a^{1/m_{2}}))-d_{\tau}(f_{1/l(1)}(a)))-\eta_{l(1)}

(e 8.43)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle nd_{\tau}(f_{1/2}(b_{1,1}))+(d_{\tau}(f_{1/k_{2}}(a^{{1/m_{2}}}))-d_{\tau}(f_{1/l(1)}(a)))-\eta_{l(1)}

(e 8.44)

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle nd_{\tau}(f_{1/2}(b_{1,1}))+\sigma_{l(1)}-\eta_{l(1)}>nd_{\tau}(f_{1/2}(b_{1,1})).

(e 8.45)

Since
A
has strict comparison, we obtain

\displaystyle[c_{1}]\leq[c_{2}].

(e 8.46)

Suppose that we have constructed
integers
k_{i},m_{i},l(i)\in\mathbb{N}
and
b_{i,1}\in\mathrm{Her}(a)_{+}^{\bf 1},

1\leq i\leq I

such that, for all
2\leq i\leq I
and
\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w}
(with
l(0)=1
and
\eta_{l(0)}=\eta_{1}
),

\displaystyle k_{i}>J(l(i-1))>l(i-1),

(e 8.47)

\displaystyle[f_{1/k_{i}}(a){{\widehat{]}}}(\tau)<n[f_{1/k_{i}}(b_{i,1}){{\widehat{]}}}(\tau)+\eta_{l(i-1)}\,\,\,{\rm and}\,\,\,

(e 8.48)

\displaystyle n[f_{1/k_{i}}(b_{i,1}){{\widehat{]}}}(\tau)\leq[f_{1/l(i)}(a){\widehat{]}}(\tau),

(e 8.49)

and verified that
[f_{1/k_{i}}(b_{i,1})]\leq[f_{1/k_{i+1}}(b_{i+1,1})],

1\leq i\leq I-1.

Define
c_{i}=f_{1/k_{i}}(b_{i,1}),

1\leq i\leq I.

By applying
Lemma
8.6
, there is
k_{I+1}>J(l(i))>l(i),

l(I+1)\geq k_{I+1},

m_{I+1}\geq m_{I},
and
b_{I+1,1}\in\mathrm{Her}(a)_{+}^{\bf 1}
such that,
for all
\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w},

\displaystyle[f_{1/k_{I+1}}(a^{1/m(I+1)}){\widehat{]}}(\tau)<n[f_{1/k_{I+1}}(b_{I+1,1}){\widehat{]}}(\tau)+\eta_{l(I)}\,\,\,{\rm and}\,\,\,

(e 8.50)

\displaystyle n[f_{1/k_{I+1}}(b_{I+1,1}{\widehat{]}}(\tau)\leq[f_{1/l(I+1)}(a){\widehat{]}}(\tau).

(e 8.51)

Then, for all
\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w},

\displaystyle nd_{\tau}(f_{1/k_{I+1}}(b_{I+1,1}))

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle d_{\tau}(f_{1/k_{I+1}}(a^{1/m_{I+1}}))-\eta_{l(I)}

(e 8.52)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d_{\tau}(f_{1/l(I)}(a))+(d_{\tau}(f_{1/k_{I+1}}(a^{1/m_{I+1}}))-d_{\tau}(f_{1/l(I)}(a)))-\eta_{l(I)}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle nd_{\tau}(f_{1/k_{I}}(b_{I,1}))+(d_{\tau}(f_{1/k_{I+1}}(a^{1/m_{I+1}}))-d_{\tau}(f_{1/l(I)}(a)))-\eta_{l(I)}

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle nd_{\tau}(f_{1/k_{I}}(b_{I,1}))+\sigma_{l(I)}-\eta_{l(I)}>nd_{\tau}(f_{1/k_{I}}(b_{I,1})).

Put
c_{I+1}=f_{1/k_{I+1}}(b_{I+1,1}).
Then, by
the strict comparison, estimates above imply that

\displaystyle[c_{I}]\leq[c_{I+1}].

(e 8.53)

Thus, by induction, we obtain an increasing sequence
c_{i}\in\mathrm{Her}(a)_{+}^{\bf 1}

such that, for all
\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w},

\displaystyle[f_{1/k_{i}}(a){\widehat{]}}(\tau)<n\widehat{[c_{i}]}(\tau)+\eta_{l(i-1)}\,\,\,{\rm and}\,\,\,

(e 8.54)

\displaystyle n\widehat{[c_{i}]}(\tau)\leq[f_{1/l(i)}(a){\widehat{]}}(\tau),\,\,\,i\geq 2.

(e 8.55)

Let
c\in(A\otimes{\cal K})_{+}
be such that
[c]
is the supremum of
\{[c_{i}]\}.

Then, by (
e 8.54
), for all
i,

\displaystyle d_{\tau}(f_{1/k_{i}}(a)){{<}}n\widehat{[c]}(\tau)+\eta_{l{{(i-1)}}}\leq n\widehat{[c]}(\tau)+\sigma_{0}/2^{i+1}(n+1)\,\,\,{\rm for\,\,\,all}\,\,\,\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w}.

(e 8.56)

Thus (recall that
A
is simple), for all sufficiently large
i,

\displaystyle d_{\tau}(f_{1/k_{i}}(a)){{<}}(n+1)\widehat{[c]}(\tau)\,\,\,{\rm for\,\,\,all}\,\,\,\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w}.

(e 8.57)

It follows that (let
i\to\infty
)

\displaystyle d_{\tau}(a)\leq(n+1)\widehat{[c]}(\tau)\,\,\,{\rm for\,\,\,all}\,\,\,\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w}.

(e 8.58)

On the other hand, by (
e 8.55
)

\displaystyle n\widehat{[c_{i}]}\leq\widehat{[a]}\,\,\,{\rm for\,\,\,all}\,\,\,i\in\mathbb{N}.

(e 8.59)

For any
\varepsilon>0,
choose a non-zero element
e\in A_{+}
such
that
d_{\tau}(e)<\varepsilon/2
for all
\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w}.

Then, by the strict comparison,

\displaystyle n[c_{i}]\leq[a]+[e]\,\,\,{\rm for\,\,\,all}\,\,\,i\in\mathbb{N}.

(e 8.60)

If follows that

n[c]\leq[a]+[e].

Hence

\displaystyle n\widehat{[c]}<d_{\tau}(a)+\varepsilon\,\,\,{\rm for\,\,\,all}\,\,\,\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w}.

(e 8.61)

Let
\varepsilon\to 0.
We also obtain

\displaystyle n\widehat{[c]}\leq\widehat{[a]}\,\,\,{\rm for\,\,\,all}\,\,\,\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w}.

(e 8.62)

Combining (
e 8.62
) and (
e 8.58
), the claim also holds for Case (2).

We now show that the proved claim implies that
\mathrm{Cu}(A)
has the property
D stated in the proof of Proposition 6.21 of
[
40
]
.
Let
x^{\prime}\ll x,
where
x=[a]
for some
a\in(A\otimes{\cal K})_{+}.

Since

{{x=\sup\{[(a-\|a\|/k)_{+}]:k\in\mathbb{N}\},}}

then

{{x^{\prime}\leq[(a-\|a\|/k)_{+}]\,\,\,{\rm for}\,\,\,k\in\mathbb{N}.}}

Note
(a-\|a\|/k)_{+}\in{\rm Ped}(A\otimes{\cal K})_{+}.

Then the claim implies that there is
y\in\mathrm{Cu}(A)
such
that

\displaystyle\widehat{x^{\prime}}\leq[(a-\|a\|/k)_{+}{\widehat{]}}\leq(n+1)\widehat{y}\,\,\,{\rm and}\,\,\,n\widehat{y}\leq[(a-\|a\|/k)_{+}{\widehat{]}}\leq[a]=x.

(e 8.63)

Therefore, as observed by L. Robert (see Property D in the proof of Proposition 6.21
of
[
40
]
),
following Corollary 5.8 of
[
13
]
,
\Gamma
is surjective.
∎

Lemma 8.8
.

Let
A
be a
C^{*}
-algebra  and
a,b\in A_{+}^{\bf 1}.

Suppose that there is
x\in A
such that

\displaystyle x^{*}x=a\,\,\,{\rm and}\,\,\,xx^{*}\in{\rm Her}(b).

(e 8.64)

Then, for any
\varepsilon>0,
there exists a unitary
U\in M_{2}({{\widetilde{A}}})
such that

\displaystyle U^{*}{\rm diag}(f_{\varepsilon}(a),0)U\in{\rm Her}{{({\rm diag}(b,0)).}}

(e 8.65)

Proof.

First we claim that, for any
y\in A,

{\rm dist}({\rm diag}(y,0),GL(M_{2}({{\widetilde{A}}})))=0.

To see this, let
\varepsilon>0.
Choose
V=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}
which is a unitary in
M_{2}(\mathbb{C}).

Then

Y:=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}y&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
y&0\end{pmatrix}

which is a nilpotent. Therefore
Y+\varepsilon\cdot 1_{2}\in GL(M_{2}({{\widetilde{A}}})).

Then

{{{\rm diag}(y,0)\approx_{\varepsilon}V^{*}(Y+\varepsilon)\in GL(M_{2}({{\widetilde{A}}})).}}

This proves the claim.

To prove the lemma, we will combine the claim with an argument of Rørdam.
By Proposition 2.4 of
[
43
]
, for any
\varepsilon>0,
there exist
\delta>0
and
r\in A
such that

\displaystyle f_{\varepsilon/2}(a)=rf_{\delta}(b)r^{*}.

(e 8.66)

Put
z=rf_{\delta}(b)^{1/2}
and
Z={\rm diag}(z,0).

By the claim and Theorem 5 of
[
36
]
, there is a unitary
U\in M_{2}({{\widetilde{A}}})

such that

\displaystyle U^{*}f_{1/2}(ZZ^{*})U=U^{*}f_{1/2}({\rm diag}(zz^{*},0))U={\rm diag}(f_{1/2}(z^{*}z),0)=f_{1/2}(Z^{*}Z).

(e 8.67)

Note that
Z^{*}Z\in{\rm Her}({\bar{b}}),
where
{\bar{b}}={\rm diag}(b,0).

Moreover (with
{\bar{a}}={\rm diag}(a,0)
)

\displaystyle U^{*}f_{\varepsilon}({\bar{a}})U\leq U^{*}f_{1/2}(f_{\varepsilon/2}({\bar{a}}){{)U}}=U^{*}f_{1/2}(ZZ^{*})U=f_{1/2}(Z^{*}Z).

∎

Lemma 8.9
.

Let
A
be an algebraically simple
C^{*}
-algebra
which has strict comparison.
Suppose that
QT(A)\not=\emptyset
and the canonical map
\Gamma
is surjective.

Suppose that
a,a^{\prime}\in{\rm Ped}(A)^{\bf 1}\setminus\{0\}

with
a\in\mathrm{Her}(a^{\prime}).
Then, there exists
1/2>\varepsilon_{0}
satisfying the following:
for any
0<\eta<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0},
any
\sigma>0,
there exist
c\in{\rm Her}(f_{\eta}(a))_{+}^{\bf 1}

with
\|c\|\leq\|a\|
and
unitary
U\in M_{2}({\rm Her}(a^{\prime})^{\sim})

such that (with
b=U^{*}{\rm diag}(c,0)U
)

\displaystyle(1)\hskip 14.45377pt{\rm diag}(f_{\varepsilon}(a),0)\leq b,

(e 8.68)

\displaystyle(2)\hskip 14.45377ptd_{\tau}(f_{\varepsilon}(a))\leq d_{\tau}(b)\leq d_{\tau}(f_{\eta}(a))\,\,\,\text{for\,\,\,all}\,\,\,\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w},

(e 8.69)

and, for some
1>\delta>0,

\displaystyle\hskip-101.17755pt(3)

\displaystyle\,\,\,|d_{\tau}(b)-\tau(f_{\delta}(b))|<\sigma\,\,\,\text{for\,\,\,all}\,\,\,\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w}.

(e 8.70)

Moreover,

\displaystyle\hskip-173.44756pt(4)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.45377ptU^{*}{\rm diag}(g_{\eta/2}(a),a^{\prime})U\in B,

(e 8.71)

where
B:={\rm Her}(b)^{\perp}\cap{\rm Her}({\rm diag}(a,a^{\prime}))
and

g_{\eta}(t)\in C_{0}((0,1])
is defined as in
2.5
.

Consequently, if
e

is a strictly positive element in
({\rm Her}(b)^{\perp}\cap{\rm Her}({\rm diag}(a,a^{\prime}))),

then

\displaystyle d_{\tau}(e)>d_{\tau}(a^{\prime})+d_{\tau}(g_{\eta}(a))\,\,\,\text{for\,\,\,all}\,\,\,\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w}.

(e 8.72)

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\|a\|\leq 1.

Let us first assume that
[0,\varepsilon_{0})\subset{\rm sp}(a)
for some
\varepsilon_{0}>0.

Fix
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}.

Note that, without loss of generality, we may assume that

\displaystyle d_{\tau}(f_{\varepsilon}(a))<\tau(f_{\delta_{1}}(a))<d_{\tau}(f_{\eta_{1}}(a))<\tau(f_{\delta_{2}}(a))<d_{\tau}(f_{\eta}(a))\,\,\,{\rm for\,\,\,all}\,\,\,\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w},

(e 8.73)

where
\varepsilon/2>\delta_{1},\delta_{1}/2>\eta_{1},\eta_{1}/2>\delta_{2},\delta_{2}/2>\eta.

Put
h_{i}(\tau)=\tau(f_{\delta_{i}}(a))\,\,\,{\rm for\,\,\,all}\,\,\,\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w},

i=1,2.

Then
h_{i}\in\operatorname{Aff}_{+}(\overline{QT(A)}^{w}),

i=1,2.

Since
\Gamma
is surjective, there is
c_{0}\in(A\otimes{\cal K})_{+}
such
that
d_{\tau}(c_{0})=h_{2}(\tau)
for all
\tau\in{{\overline{QT(A)}^{w}.}}

Choose
\delta_{0}>0
such that (as
h_{2}
is continuous)

\displaystyle d_{\tau}(c_{0})-\tau(f_{\delta_{0}}(c_{0}))<\sigma/2\,\,\,\text{for\,\,\,all}\,\,\,\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w}.

(e 8.74)

Since
h_{1}<h_{2}
are continuous, we may also assume, by choosing smaller
\delta_{0}
, that

\displaystyle d_{\tau}(f_{\delta_{0}}(c_{0}))>d_{\tau}(f_{{\eta_{1}}}(a)){{>d_{\tau}(f_{\varepsilon}(a))}}\,\,\,{\rm for\,\,\,all}\,\,\,\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w}.

(e 8.75)

Since
A
has strict comparison, by (
e 8.73
),
there is
x\in A\otimes{\cal K}
such
that

\displaystyle x^{*}x=f_{\delta_{0}/4}(c_{0})\,\,\,{\rm and}\,\,\,xx^{*}\in{\rm Her}(f_{\eta}(a)).

(e 8.76)

Choose
c=xx^{*}.
Then
0\leq c\leq 1
and
d_{\tau}(c)=d_{\tau}(f_{\delta_{0}/4}(c_{0}))
for all
\overline{QT(A)}^{w}.

Let
C={\rm Her}(f_{\eta}(a)).

By (
e 8.75
), the strict comparison and Lemma
8.8
, we obtain
a unitary
U\in M_{2}(\widetilde{C})
such that

\displaystyle U{\rm diag}(f_{\eta_{1}}(a),0)U^{*}\in{\rm Her}({\bar{c}}),

(e 8.77)

where
{\bar{c}}={\rm diag}(c,0).

Let
b=U^{*}{\bar{c}}U.
Then

\displaystyle{{{\rm diag}(f_{\varepsilon}(a),0)\leq}}{\rm diag}(f_{\eta_{1}}(a),0)\leq b.

(e 8.78)

(So (1) holds).
Moreover,

d_{\tau}(b)=d_{\tau}(c)
for all
\tau\in\overline{T(A)}^{w}.

Consequently

\displaystyle d_{\tau}(f_{\eta_{1}}(a))\leq d_{\tau}(b)\leq d_{\tau}(f_{\eta}(a))\,\,\,{\rm for\,\,\,all}\,\,\,\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w}

(e 8.79)

(so (2) holds). Moreover,
there is
1>\delta>0
such that

\displaystyle d_{\tau}(f_{\delta}(b))\geq\tau(f_{\delta_{0}}(c))\,\,\,{\rm for\,\,\,all}\,\,\,\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w}.

(e 8.80)

It then follows from (
e 8.74
) that

\displaystyle|d_{\tau}(b)-\tau(f_{\delta}(b))|=|d_{\tau}(f_{\delta_{0}/4}(c_{0}))-\tau(f_{\delta}(b))|<\sigma\,\,\,{\rm for\,\,\,all}\,\,\,\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w}

(e 8.81)

(so (3) holds). To show the “ Moreover” part,
put

{{B={\rm Her}(b)^{\perp}\cap{\rm Her}({\rm diag}(a,a^{\prime}))\,\,\,{\rm and}\,\,\,e^{\prime}=U^{*}{\rm diag}(g_{\eta/2}(a),a^{\prime})U.}}

Since

g_{\eta/2}(a)\perp f_{\eta}(a),
we have

{{{\rm diag}(g_{\eta/2}(a),a^{\prime})\perp{\rm diag}(f_{\eta}(a),0)\,\,\,{\rm and}\,\,\,e^{\prime}\perp b.}}

It follows that

U^{*}{\rm diag}(g_{\eta/2}(a),a^{\prime})U\in B.

If
e
is a strictly positive element in
{\rm Her}(b)^{\perp}\cap{\rm Her}({\rm diag}(a,a^{\prime})),
then

\displaystyle d_{\tau}(e)\geq d_{\tau}(e^{\prime})=d_{\tau}(a^{\prime})+d_{\tau}(g_{\eta/2}(a))\,\,\,{\rm for\,\,\,all}\,\,\,\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w}.

(e 8.82)

This proves the case that
[0,\varepsilon_{0}]\subset{\rm sp}(a).

If there exists
r_{n}\in(0,1]
with

{{r_{n}>r_{n+1}\,\,\,{\rm and}\,\,\,\lim_{n\to\infty}r_{n}=0\,\,\,{\rm such\,\,that}\,\,\,r_{n}\not\in{\rm sp}(a),}}

then
b_{n}=f_{2r_{n}}(a)
has the property that
\omega(b_{n})=0.

Then the lemma follows by choosing
U={\rm diag}(1,1)
and

b=b_{n}
for some sufficiently large
n.

∎

Lemma 8.10
.

Let
A
be a
\sigma
-unital algebraically simple
C^{*}
-algebra  with
QT(A){{\not=}}\emptyset.

Suppose that
A
has strict comparison and
\Gamma
is surjective.
Suppose that
a={\rm diag}(0,a_{1},a_{2},...,a_{n})
in
M_{n+1}(A){{{}^{\bf 1}_{+}}}
for some integer
n\geq 1.

Then, for any
1/2>\varepsilon>0
and
1/2>\sigma>0,
there exists
d\in M_{n+1}(A){{{}^{\bf 1}_{+}}}

such that

\displaystyle f_{\varepsilon}(a)\leq d{{\leq 1}}\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\omega(d)<\sigma.

(e 8.83)

Proof.

For
n=1,
this follows immediately from Lemma

8.9
.

Assume that the lemma holds for
n\geq 1.

Let
0\leq e_{A}\leq 1
be a strictly positive element of
A.

Fix
1/2>\varepsilon>0.

Choose
\eta=\varepsilon/4(n+2)
and
\sigma_{0}:=\sigma/2(n+2).

We will apply Lemma
8.9
with
a_{j}\in\mathrm{Her}(e_{A})
(
1\leq j\leq n+1
),

\eta
as above, and
\sigma_{0}
(in place of
\sigma
).

By Lemma
8.9
,
there is
c_{1}\in{\rm Her}(f_{\eta}(a_{1}))_{+}^{\bf 1},
a unitary
U_{1}\in M_{2}({{\widetilde{A}}})
and
{{b_{1}^{\prime}}}=U_{1}^{*}{\rm diag}(0,c_{1})U_{1}

such that

\displaystyle{\rm diag}(0,f_{\varepsilon}(a_{1}))\leq b_{1}^{\prime},

(e 8.84)

\displaystyle d_{\tau}(f_{\varepsilon}(a_{1}))\leq d_{\tau}(b_{1}^{\prime})\leq d_{\tau}(f_{\eta}(a_{1}))\,\,\,\text{for\,\,\,all}\,\,\,\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w},

(e 8.85)

\displaystyle\omega(b_{1})<\sigma_{0}\,\,\,{\rm and}\,\,\,U_{1}^{*}{\rm diag}(e_{A},g_{\eta/2}(a_{1}))U_{1}\in B_{1},

(e 8.86)

where
B_{1}:=({\rm Her}({{b_{1}^{\prime}}})^{\perp}\cap{\rm Her}({\rm diag}(a_{1},e_{A})))_{+}.

Put

\displaystyle V_{1}={\rm diag}(U_{1},\overbrace{1_{{{\widetilde{A}}}},...,1_{{{\widetilde{A}}}}}^{n-1}),\,\,{\alpha}_{2}=V_{1}^{*}{\rm diag}(0,0,a_{2},...,a_{n+1})V_{1}\,\,\,{\rm and}\,\,\,

(e 8.87)

\displaystyle{{b_{1}}}=V_{1}^{*}(0,c_{1},0,...,0)V_{1}.

(e 8.88)

Define
C_{1}={\rm Her}(V_{1}^{*}(e_{A},0,e_{A},...,e_{A})V_{1}).

Then

\displaystyle b_{1}\in C_{1}^{\perp}\,\,\,{\rm and}\,\,\,C_{1}\cong M_{n+1}(A).

(e 8.89)

In
C_{1},
we may write
{\alpha}_{2}={\rm diag}(0,a_{2},a_{3},...,a_{n+1})

(the number of possible nonzero elements is now reduced to
n
).

By the induction assumption, there is
b_{2}\in C_{1}
with
0\leq b_{2}\leq 1
such that

\displaystyle f_{\varepsilon}({\alpha}_{2})\leq b_{2}\,\,\,{\rm and}\,\,\,\omega(b_{2})<\sigma_{0}.

(e 8.90)

Define
d:=b_{1}+b_{2}.
Note that
b_{1}\perp b_{2}.

Then

\displaystyle f_{\varepsilon}(a)={\rm diag}(0,f_{\varepsilon}(a_{1}),f_{\varepsilon}(a_{2}),...,f_{\varepsilon}(a_{n+1}))\leq b_{1}+b_{2}.

(e 8.91)

and (by (2) of
4.4
),

\displaystyle\omega(d)\leq\omega(b_{1})+\omega(b_{2})<2\sigma_{0}<\sigma.

(e 8.92)

This completes the induction. The lemma follows.

∎

Theorem 8.11
.

Let
A
be a
\sigma
-unital simple
C^{*}
-algebra  with

\widetilde{QT}(A)\setminus\{0\}\not=\emptyset.

Suppose that
A
has strict comparison
and property (TM). Then

A
has
T
-tracial approximate oscillation zero.

Proof.

Choose
e\in{\rm Ped}(A)_{+}\setminus\{0\}
and
A_{1}=\mathrm{Her}(e).

Then
{\rm Ped}(A_{1})=A_{1}.

To prove the theorem, without loss of generality, we may assume that

A={\rm Ped}(A).

By Theorem
8.7
,
\Gamma
is surjective.

Fix
a\in{\rm Ped}(A\otimes{\cal K})_{+}^{\bf 1}.

We claim that
{\rm Her}(a)
has a T-tracial approximate identity
\{d_{n}\}

such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\omega(d_{n})=0.

Put
B=\mathrm{Her}(a).
Then
{\rm Ped}(B)=B
and
B\otimes{\cal K}\cong A\otimes{\cal K}.

Therefore, to simplify notation, without loss of generality, we may also assume that
a\in A.

Let
\varepsilon>0
and let
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
1/n<(\varepsilon/8)^{2}.

Since
A
has property (TM), there is a c.p.c. order zero map
\varphi:M_{n+1}\to{\rm Her}(a)
such that

\displaystyle\|a-\varphi(1_{n+1})a\|_{{}_{2,\overline{QT(A)}^{w}}}<(\varepsilon/8)^{3}.

(e 8.93)

By Proposition
8.3
, let
{{C}}={\rm Her}(\varphi(1_{n+1}))\cong M_{n+1}(({\rm Her}(\varphi(e_{1,1}))).

Write

\displaystyle\varphi(1_{n+1})={\rm diag}(c,c,...,c)\in M_{n+1}({\rm Her}(\varphi(e_{1,1}))).

(e 8.94)

Choose
0<\eta<(\varepsilon/16)^{2}.

Put
c_{n}={\rm diag}(0,c,c,...,c).

It follows from Lemma
8.10
that there exists
d\in{{C}}_{+}^{\bf 1}

such that

\displaystyle f_{\eta}(c_{n})\leq d\leq 1\,\,\,{\rm and}\,\,\,\omega(d)<1/2^{n}.

(e 8.95)

Hence

\displaystyle 0\leq({c_{n}-2\eta})_{+}(1-d)({c_{n}-2\eta})_{+}\leq({{c_{n}}}-2\eta)_{+}(1-f_{\eta}(c_{n}))({{c_{n}}}-2\eta)_{+}=0.

(e 8.96)

Hence
d(c_{n}-2\eta)_{+}=(c_{n}-2\eta)_{+}=(c_{n}-2\eta)_{+}d.

It follows that (we now working in a commutative
C^{*}
-subalgebra)

\displaystyle(1-d)^{2}\leq(1-({{c_{n}-2\eta}})_{+})^{2}.

(e 8.97)

Note that

\displaystyle\|\varphi(1_{n+1})-c_{n}\|_{{}_{2,\overline{QT(A)}^{w}}}<{1\over{n+1}}.

(e 8.98)

Then (see also (
2.16
)), by (
e 8.97
), (
e 8.98
) and by (
e 8.93
),

\displaystyle\|a-da\|^{2/3}_{{}_{2,\overline{QT(A)}^{w}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\sup\,_{{}_{\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w}}}\{\tau(a(1-d)^{2}a)\})^{1/3}

(e 8.99)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sup\,_{{}_{\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w}}}\{\tau(a(1-(c_{n}-2\eta)_{+})^{2}a)\})^{1/3}=\|a-(c_{n}-2\eta)_{+}a\|^{2/3}_{{}_{2,\overline{QT(A)}^{w}}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|a-c_{n}a\|^{2/3}_{{}_{2,\overline{QT(A)}^{w}}}+\|(c_{n}-({c_{n}-2\eta})_{+})a\|^{2/3}_{{}_{2,\overline{QT(A)}^{w}}}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\|a-\varphi(1_{n+1})a\|^{2/3}_{{}_{2,\overline{QT(A)}^{w}}}+({1\over{n+1}})^{2/3}+(2\eta)^{2/3}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle(\varepsilon/8)^{2}+(\varepsilon/8)^{2}+(\varepsilon/8)^{2}=3(\varepsilon/8)^{2}.

(e 8.100)

Since
\omega(d)<1/2^{n},

this proves the claim.
The theorem then follows from the claim and Lemma

5.6
.
∎

9
Stable rank one

Let
A
be a
C^{*}
-algebra
and
n\in\mathbb{N}.

Recall that we view
M_{n}(A)

as a
C^{*}
-subalgebra of
M_{n+1}(A)

in the canonical way, i.e.,

M_{n}(A)
is the upper left block of
M_{n+1}(A).

Recall an element
a=(a_{i,j})_{n\times n}
in
M_{n}(A)
is called upper
(resp. lower) triangular, if

a_{i,j}=0
whenever
i<j
(resp.
i>j
),
and,
a
is called
strictly upper (resp. lower) triangular,
if
a_{i,j}=0
whenever
i\leq j
(resp.
i\geq j
).

The following proposition is a generalization of an
elementary fact
in linear algebra.

Proposition 9.1
.

Let
A
be a
C^{*}
-algebra  such that
A\subset\overline{GL({{\widetilde{A}}})}.

Then, for any
n\in\mathbb{N},
any
a\in M_{n}(A),

any
\varepsilon>0,

there is an upper triangular matrix
x\in M_{n}(A)

and a lower triangular matrix
y\in M_{n}(A)

such that
a\approx_{\varepsilon}xy.

Proof.

We prove this by induction on
n.

For
n=1,
let

a\in M_{n}(A)=A
and

\varepsilon>0.

By the existence of approximate identity,
there is
e\in A_{+}

such that
a\approx_{\varepsilon}ae.
Note that
a
and
e

are triangular matrices in
M_{n}(A).

Thus the proposition holds for
n=1.

Assume the proposition holds for
n\geq 1.

Let
a=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n+1}a_{i,j}\otimes e_{i,j}\in A\otimes M_{n+1},

where
a_{i,j}\in A
and
\{e_{i,j}\}

is the matrix units of
M_{n+1},

i,j=1,...,n+1.

Let
\varepsilon>0.

Since
A\subset\overline{GL({{\widetilde{A}}})},

there is
\tilde{a}\in GL(\widetilde{A})

such that

{{a_{n+1,n+1}\approx_{\varepsilon/2}\tilde{a}.}}

In what follows in this proof,
1
is the identity of

{{\widetilde{A}}}
and
1_{n+1}
is the identity
of
M_{n+1}({{\widetilde{A}}}).

Let
b^{(0)}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i,n+1}\tilde{a}^{-1}\otimes e_{i,n+1}
and
c^{(0)}:=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\tilde{a}^{-1}a_{n+1,j}\otimes e_{n+1,j}.
Then
b^{(0)}
and
c^{(0)}
are nilpotents.
Put

\displaystyle a^{\prime}:=a+(\tilde{a}-a_{n+1,n+1})\otimes e_{n+1,n+1},

(e 9.1)

\displaystyle b:=1_{n+1}-b^{(0)}\quad\text{and}\quad c:=1_{n+1}-c^{(0)}.

(e 9.2)

Let

s:=ba^{\prime}c.

Note that
a^{\prime},b,c,s
are in
M_{n+1}(\widetilde{A}).

Let
a^{\prime}_{i,j}
(resp.
b_{i,j},c_{i,j},s_{i,j}
)
be the
(i,j)
-th entry of

a^{\prime}
(resp.
b,c,s
),
1\leq i,j\leq n+1.

Note that

\displaystyle s_{i,j}=\sum_{m=1}^{n+1}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n+1}b_{i,k}a^{\prime}_{k,m}c_{m,j}\right)\quad(1\leq i,j\leq n+1).

(e 9.3)

If
A=\widetilde{A},
then
s_{i,j}\in A.

Otherwise denote by
\pi:M_{n+1}({{\widetilde{A}}})\to M_{n+1}
the quotient
map. Then

{{\pi(b)=\pi(1_{n+1})=\pi(c)\,\,\,{\rm and}\,\,\,\pi({{a^{\prime}}})=\pi(\tilde{a}\otimes e_{n+1,n+1}).}}

It follows that
\pi(s)=\pi(\tilde{a}\otimes e_{n+1,n+1}).

Thus

\displaystyle s_{i,j}\in A\quad(1\leq i,j\leq n).

(e 9.4)

Note that

b_{n+1,k}=0
for
1\leq k\leq n

and
c_{m,j}=0
for
m\notin\{j,n+1\},
if
1\leq j\leq n.

By
(
e 9.3
), we have, for
1\leq j\leq n,

\displaystyle s_{n+1,j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle b_{n+1,n+1}a^{\prime}_{n+1,j}c_{j,j}+b_{n+1,n+1}a^{\prime}_{n+1,n+1}c_{n+1,j}

(e 9.5)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a_{n+1,j}+\tilde{a}\cdot(-\tilde{a}^{-1}a_{n+1,j})=0.

(e 9.6)

If

1\leq i\leq n,
then

b_{i,k}=0
for
k\notin\{i,n+1\}

and

c_{m,n+1}=0
for
1\leq m\leq n.

By (
e 9.3
), we compute

\displaystyle s_{i,n+1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle b_{i,i}a^{\prime}_{i,n+1}c_{n+1,n+1}+b_{i,n+1}a^{\prime}_{n+1,n+1}c_{n+1,n+1}

(e 9.7)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a_{i,n+1}+(-a_{i,n+1}\tilde{a}^{-1})\cdot\tilde{a}=0.

(e 9.8)

We also have

\displaystyle s_{n+1,n+1}=\sum_{m=1}^{n+1}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n+1}b_{n+1,k}a^{\prime}_{k,m}c_{m,n+1}\right)=b_{n+1,n+1}a^{\prime}_{n+1,n+1}c_{n+1,n+1}=\tilde{a}.

(e 9.9)

Therefore
(
e 9.4
), (
e 9.6
), (
e 9.8
),
and (
e 9.9
) show that

{{ba^{\prime}c=d+\tilde{a}\otimes e_{n+1,n+1},}}

where
d\in M_{n}(A).
Note that
b
and
c
are invertible in
M_{n+1}(\widetilde{A}),

as both
b^{(0)}
and
c^{(0)}
are nilpotents.
Let
\varepsilon_{1}=\frac{\varepsilon}{4(1+\|b^{-1}\|\cdot\|c^{-1}\|)}.

By our assumption,
there is an upper triangular matrix
x_{1}

and a lower triangular matrix
y_{1}
in
M_{n}(A)

such that

\displaystyle d\approx_{\varepsilon_{1}}x_{1}y_{1}.

(e 9.10)

Let
e\in M_{n+1}(A)_{+}^{\bf 1}

be a diagonal matrix
such that

a\approx_{\varepsilon/4}eae.

Note that

b^{-1}=1_{n+1}+b^{(0)}

and

x{{:}}=eb^{-1}(x_{1}+\tilde{a}\otimes e_{n+1,n+1})

are upper triangular matrix in
M_{n+1}(A).

Similarly,

c^{-1}{{=1_{n+1}+c^{(0)}}}
is a lower
triangular matrix in
M_{n+1}(\widetilde{A}),

and

{{y:=(y_{1}+{{1}}\otimes 1_{n+1,n+1})c^{-1}e}}

is a lower triangular matrix in
M_{n+1}(A).

Then

\displaystyle a\approx_{\varepsilon/4}eae

\displaystyle\approx_{\varepsilon/2}

\displaystyle ea^{\prime}e=eb^{-1}ba^{\prime}cc^{-1}e=eb^{-1}(d+\tilde{a}\otimes e_{n+1,n+1})c^{-1}e

(e 9.11)

\displaystyle\approx_{\varepsilon/4}

\displaystyle eb^{-1}(x_{1}y_{1}+\tilde{a}\otimes e_{n+1,n+1})c^{-1}e

(e 9.12)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle eb^{-1}(x_{1}+\tilde{a}\otimes e_{n+1,n+1})\cdot(y_{1}+1\otimes e_{n+1,n+1})c^{-1}e=xy.

(e 9.13)

Thus the proposition holds for
n+1.

By induction, the proposition holds.
∎

Proposition 9.2
.

Let
A
be a
C^{*}
-algebra  such that
A\subset\overline{GL({{\widetilde{A}}})}

and let
n\in\mathbb{N}.

Then for any
a\in M_{n}(A)
and
any
\varepsilon>0,

there is a strictly upper triangular matrix

x\in M_{n+1}(A)
and a
strictly lower triangular matrix
y\in M_{n+1}(A)

such that
a\approx_{\varepsilon}xy.

In particular,
any element in
M_{n}(A)

can be approximated in norm by product of two nilpotent
elements in
M_{n+1}(A).

Proof.

By Proposition
9.1
,
there is an upper triangular matrix
x_{1}\in M_{n}(A)

and a lower triangular matrix
y_{1}\in M_{n}(A)

such that
a\approx_{\varepsilon}x_{1}y_{1}.

Let
v=\sum_{i=1}^{n}1_{\widetilde{A}}\otimes e_{i,i+1}\in M_{n+1}(\widetilde{A}).

Then
x=x_{1}v\in M_{n+1}(A)
is a strict upper triangular matrix
and
y=v^{*}y_{1}\in M_{n+1}(A)
is a strict lower triangular matrix,
and
xy=x_{1}vv^{*}y_{1}=x_{1}y_{1}\approx_{\varepsilon}a
(Recall that
we identify
M_{n}(A)
with the
upper left
n\times n
corner
of
M_{n+1}(A)
).

The last part of the proposition follows
from the fact that strictly triangular matrices
are nilpotents.
∎

Lemma 9.3
.

Let
A
be a
\sigma
-unital algebraically simple non-elementary
C^{*}
-algebra with
QT(A)\not=\emptyset

which has
strict comparison.
Suppose that
A
also has the
property (TM).
Let
a\in A.
If there are
b_{1},b_{2}\in A_{+}\setminus\{0\}

such that

a^{*}a+aa^{*},b_{1},b_{2}
are mutually orthogonal,
then, for any
\varepsilon>0,

there are two nilpotents
x,y\in A

such that
\|a-xy\|<\varepsilon.

Proof.

Let
B=l^{\infty}(A)/I_{{}_{\overline{QT(A)}^{w}}}.

Recall that
\Pi:l^{\infty}(A)\to B
is the quotient map and

\iota:A\to l^{\infty}(A)
is the canonical embedding.
Denote
{\bar{\iota}}:=\Pi\circ\iota.

Fix
a\in A.

Without loss of generality, we may assume
\|a\|\leq 1.

Put
a_{0}=a^{*}a+aa^{*}.

Assume that there are
b_{1},b_{2}\in A_{+}

such that
0=b_{1}b_{2}=ab_{1}=b_{1}a=ab_{2}=b_{2}a{{}}.

Let
\varepsilon>0.
Since
A
is simple and non-elementary, one
can choose
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that

{{1/n<\inf\{d_{\tau}(b_{i}):\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w}\}}},\,\,\,i=1,2.

Since
A
has property (TM), by Theorem
8.11
,
A
has T-tracial approximate oscillation zero.
Then, by Theorem
6.6
,
B
has stable rank one.
Also, by the last part of Remark
8.2
,
for each
m\in\mathbb{N},

there is a c.p.c. order zero map

\varphi_{m}:M_{n}\to\mathrm{Her}({{a_{0}}})
such that

\displaystyle\|a-\varphi_{m}(1_{n})a\|_{{}_{2,\overline{QT(A)}^{w}}}<1/m.

(e 9.14)

Let
\varphi:M_{n}\to l^{\infty}(A)

be the map induced by
\{\varphi_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
and

{\bar{\varphi}}:=\Pi\circ\varphi.

Then (
e 9.14
)
shows that

\displaystyle{{{\bar{\varphi}}(1_{n}){\bar{\iota}}(a)={\bar{\iota}}(a).}}

(e 9.15)

Denote by
\{e_{i,j}:1\leq i,j\leq n\}
a system of matrix units for
M_{n}

and
\{e_{i,j}:1\leq{{i}},j\leq n+1\}
an expanded system of matrix units for
M_{n+1}.

In particular we view
M_{n}
generated by
\{e_{i,j}:1\leq i,j\leq 1\}
as a
C^{*}
-subalgebra  of
M_{n+1}
generated by
\{e_{i,j}:1\leq i,j\leq n+1\}.

Since
A
has strict comparison, and
for all
m\in\mathbb{N},

\displaystyle\sup\{d_{\tau}(\varphi_{m}(e_{1,1})):\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w}\}\leq 1/n<\inf\{d_{\tau}(b_{2}):\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w}\},

(e 9.16)

we have
\varphi_{m}(e_{1,1})\lesssim b_{2}
for all
m\in\mathbb{N}.

By
[
43
, Proposition 2.4 (iv)]
,
there are

{v_{m}}\in A

\displaystyle v_{m}^{*}v_{m}=(\varphi_{m}(e_{1,1})-1/m)_{+}\quad\text{ and }\quad v_{m}v_{m}^{*}\in\mathrm{Her}_{A}(b_{2})\quad(m\in\mathbb{N})

(e 9.17)

(see (
e 3.5
)) and (
e 3.6
)).
Note that

{{\|v_{m}\|^{2}=\|v_{m}^{*}v_{m}\|=\|(\varphi_{m}(e_{1,1})-1/m)_{+}\|\leq 1.}}

Let
v=\{v_{1},v_{2},...\}\in l^{\infty}(A).

Since

\|(\varphi_{m}(e_{1,1})-1/m)_{+}-\varphi_{m}(e_{1,1})\|\leq 1/m

(
m\in\mathbb{N}
),
we have

\displaystyle{{\Pi}}(v^{*}v)={\bar{\varphi}}(e_{1,1}).

(e 9.18)

The facts that
\varphi_{m}(1_{n})\in\mathrm{Her}({{a_{0}}}),

\mathrm{Her}({{a_{0}}})\bot\mathrm{Her}(b_{2}),
and

v_{m}v_{m}^{*}\in\mathrm{Her}(b_{2})
show that

\varphi_{m}(1_{n})\bot v_{m}v_{m}^{*}
for all
m\in\mathbb{N}.

Hence

\displaystyle\Pi(vv^{*}){\bar{\varphi}}(1_{n})=0.

(e 9.19)

Let
h:C_{0}((0,1])\otimes M_{n}\to B
the homomorphism  defined by

h(\imath\otimes e_{i,j})={\bar{\varphi}}(e_{i,j})
(
1\leq i,j\leq n
). Extend

{\widetilde{h}}:C_{0}((0,1])\otimes M_{n+1}\to B
by

\widetilde{h}(\imath\otimes e_{i,j})=h({{\imath\otimes e_{i,j}}})
and

\widetilde{h}(\imath\otimes e_{1,n+1})=v^{*}.
By (
e 9.18
) and (
e 9.19
),

\widetilde{h}
is indeed a homomorphism. Define
\widetilde{\varphi}(e_{i,j})=\widetilde{h}({{\imath\otimes e_{i,j}}})
for

1\leq i,j\leq n+1.

As we view
M_{n}
as
C^{*}
-subalgebra  of
M_{n+1},

\widetilde{\varphi}
is an extension of
{\bar{\varphi}},
i.e.,
\widetilde{\varphi}|_{M_{n}}={\bar{\varphi}}.

By Proposition
8.3
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Her}_{B}(\widetilde{\varphi}(1_{n+1}))\cong\mathrm{Her}_{B}(\widetilde{\varphi}(e_{1,1}))\otimes M_{n+1}=\mathrm{Her}_{B}({\bar{\varphi}}(e_{1,1}))\otimes M_{n+1},

(e 9.20)

and

\displaystyle\mathrm{Her}_{B}({\bar{\varphi}}(1_{n}))\cong\mathrm{Her}_{B}({\bar{\varphi}}(e_{1,1}))\otimes M_{n}.

(e 9.21)

Moreover, as
\{e_{i,j}:1\leq i,j\leq n\}\subset\{e_{i,j}:1\leq i,j\leq n+1\},

we also write

\mathrm{Her}_{B}({\bar{\varphi}}(1_{n}))\subset\mathrm{Her}_{B}(\widetilde{\varphi}(1_{n+1})).

Since
B
has stable rank one, by
[
6
, Corollary 3.6]
,

\mathrm{Her}_{B}(\widetilde{\varphi}(1_{n+1}))
also has stable rank one.
Note, by (
e 9.15
),
{\bar{\iota}}(a)\in\mathrm{Her}_{B}({\bar{\varphi}}(1_{n}))\cong\mathrm{Her}_{B}({\bar{\varphi}}(e_{1,1}))\otimes M_{n},

Then by Proposition
9.2
,
there are nilpotents

{{x_{1},y_{1}\in\mathrm{Her}_{B}(\widetilde{\varphi}(1_{n+1})){{\cong\mathrm{Her}_{B}({\bar{\varphi}}(e_{1,1}))\otimes M_{n+1}}}\,\,\,{\rm{such\,\,\,that}}\,\,\,\|{\bar{\iota}}(a)-x_{1}y_{1}\|<\varepsilon/8.}}

Recall that

\Pi(vv^{*}+\varphi(1_{n}))=\widetilde{\varphi}(1_{n+1}).

Thus

\displaystyle\mathrm{Her}_{B}(\widetilde{\varphi}(1_{n+1}))=\Pi(\mathrm{Her}_{l^{\infty}(A)}(vv^{*}+\varphi(1_{n}))).

(e 9.22)

Also note that
(vv^{*}+\varphi(1_{n}))\bot\iota(b_{1}).

Thus

\displaystyle\mathrm{Her}_{l^{\infty}(A)}(vv^{*}+\varphi(1_{n}))\subset\{\iota(b_{1})\}^{\bot}.

(e 9.23)

By (
e 9.22
)
and the fact that
nilpotents can be lifted
(see
[
32
, Theorem 6.7]
),
there are nilpotents
x_{2},y_{2}\in\mathrm{Her}_{l^{\infty}(A)}(vv^{*}+\varphi(1_{n}))

such that

\displaystyle\Pi(x_{2})=x_{1},\quad\Pi(y_{2})=y_{1}.

(e 9.24)

It follows from (
e 9.23
) that we also have

\displaystyle x_{2}\bot\iota(b_{1}),\quad\text{ and }\quad y_{2}\bot\iota(b_{1}).

(e 9.25)

Since
\|{\bar{\iota}}(a)-x_{1}y_{1}\|<\varepsilon/8,

there is
\bar{z}\in I_{\overline{QT(A)}^{w}}

such that

{{\iota(a)-x_{2}y_{2}\approx_{\varepsilon/8}{\bar{z}}.}}

Note that
\iota(a)-x_{2}y_{2}\in\{\iota(b_{1})\}^{\bot}.

Hence there is
d\in\{\iota(b_{1})\}^{\bot}
such that

\iota(a)-x_{2}y_{2}\approx_{\varepsilon/8}d(\iota(a)-x_{2}y_{2})d\approx_{\varepsilon/8}d\bar{z}d.

Let

\displaystyle z:=d\bar{z}d\in\{\iota(b_{1})\}^{\bot}\cap{{I_{{}_{\overline{QT(A)}^{w}}}}}.

(e 9.26)

Then

{{\|\iota(a)-(x_{2}y_{2}+z)\|<\varepsilon/4.}}

Choose
\delta>0
such that
\|zf_{\delta}(|z|)-z\|<\varepsilon/8.

Then

\displaystyle\|\iota(a)-(x_{2}y_{2}+zf_{\delta}(|z|))\|<\varepsilon/2.

(e 9.27)

Write
z=\{z_{1},z_{2},...\}
with
z_{i}\perp b_{1}

(i\in\mathbb{N}).

Since
z\in{{I_{{}_{\overline{QT(A)}^{w}}}}},
there is
i\in\mathbb{N}

such that
(note, the first inequality of the following always holds):

\displaystyle\sup_{\tau\in{{\overline{QT(A)}^{w}}}}\{d_{\tau}(f_{\delta/2}(|z_{i}|))\}\leq\frac{{4}}{\delta}\sup_{\tau\in{{\overline{QT(A)}^{w}}}}\tau(|z_{i}|)<{{\inf_{\tau\in\overline{QT(A)}^{w}}\{d_{\tau}(b_{1})\}.}}

(e 9.28)

Since
A
has strict comparison,

f_{\delta/2}(|z_{i}|)\lesssim b_{1}.

By
[
43
, Proposition 2.4 (iv)]
,
there is
r\in A
such that

\displaystyle r^{*}r=f_{\delta}(|z_{i}|)\quad\text{and}\quad rr^{*}\in\mathrm{Her}(b_{1}).

(e 9.29)

Write
x_{2}=\{x_{2,j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
and
y_{2}=\{y_{2,j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}.

By (
e 9.25
),
x_{2,i}\bot b_{1}

and
y_{2,i}\bot b_{1}.

Together with (
e 9.29
),
we have

\displaystyle x_{2,i}r=r^{*}x_{2,i}=y_{2,i}r=r^{*}y_{2,i}=0.

(e 9.30)

Thus

\displaystyle x_{2,i}y_{2,i}+z_{i}f_{\delta}(|z_{i}|)=x_{2,i}y_{2,i}+z_{i}r^{*}r=(x_{2,i}+z_{i}r^{*})(y_{2,i}+r).

(e 9.31)

Since
x_{2}
and
y_{2}
are nilpotents, so are

x_{2,i},y_{2,i},

By (
e 9.26
) and
(
e 9.29
),
r^{*}z_{i}=0.

Hence

{{(z_{i}r^{*})^{2}=z_{i}r^{*}z_{i}r^{*}=0.}}

By (
e 9.29
),
r^{2}=0.

By (
e 9.30
),

{{(z_{i}r^{*})x_{2,i}=0\,\,\,{\rm and}\,\,\,y_{2,i}r=0.}}

Let
{\alpha}_{1}:=x_{2,i},

{\alpha}_{2}:=z_{i}r^{*},

{\beta}_{1}:=y_{2,i},

and

{\beta}_{2}:=r.

Then the last paragraph shows that

{\alpha}_{1},{\alpha}_{2},{\beta}_{1},{\beta}_{2}
are all nilpotents,
and
{\alpha}_{2}{\alpha}_{1}={\beta}_{1}{\beta}_{2}=0.

Then it is standard to conclude
that
x:={\alpha}_{1}+{\alpha}_{2}
and

{y:=}{\beta}_{1}+{\beta}_{2}

are nilpotents
(see the proof of Claim 1 in the proof of

[
16
, Lemma 5.6]
).

By (
e 9.27
) and (
e 9.31
),

\displaystyle a\approx_{\varepsilon/2}x_{2,i}y_{2,i}+z_{i}f_{\delta}(|z_{i}|)=(x_{2,i}+z_{i}r^{*})(y_{2,i}+r){{=xy}}.

(e 9.32)

The lemma follows.
∎

Theorem 9.4
.

Let
A
be a

\sigma
-unital
simple
C^{*}
-algebra with
{\widetilde{QT}}(A)\setminus\{0\}\not=\emptyset.

If
A
has strict comparison and has
T
-tracial approximate oscillation zero,
then
A
has stable rank one.

Proof.

We may assume that
A
is non-elementary.
There are two cases.

Case 1.
A
has a nonzero projection
p
.

Set
A_{1}:=pAp.
Then

A_{1}
is unital, simple,
has nonempty
{{QT(A_{1})}},
and
has strict comparison as well as T-tracial approximate oscillation zero.
Hence

l^{\infty}(A_{1})/I_{{}_{\overline{QT(A_{1})}}}
has stable rank one
(see Theorem
6.6
).
Let
a\in A_{1}
be a non-invertible element and let
\varepsilon>0.

Since
A_{1}
is simple and finite,
by
[
42
, Proposition 3.2, Lemma 3.5]
,
there is a unitary
u\in U(A_{1}),

an element
\bar{a}\in A_{1},

and a positive element
b\in(A_{1})_{+}\backslash\{0\}

such that
\|a-\bar{a}\|<\varepsilon/4

and
b(u\bar{a})=(u\bar{a})b=0.

Note that
\mathrm{Her}(b)
is also infinite dimensional.
Hence there are two nonzero orthogonal positive elements

b_{1},b_{2}\in\mathrm{Her}(b).

By Theorem
8.5
,
A
has property (TM).
We then apply Lemma
9.3

to obtain two nilpotent elements

x,y
such that

{{u\bar{a}\approx_{\varepsilon/4}xy.}}

Let
\delta>0
be such that

{{xy\approx_{\varepsilon/4}(x+\delta)(y+\delta).}}

Note that
x+\delta
and
y+\delta
are invertible
since
x,y
are nilpotents.
Then that

{{a\approx_{\varepsilon/4}u^{*}u\bar{a}\approx_{\varepsilon/2}u^{*}(x+\delta)(y+\delta)}}

shows that
a
can be approximated by
an invertible element

u^{*}(x+\delta)(y+\delta)
up to the tolerance
\varepsilon.

Hence
A_{1}
has stable rank one.
It follows that
A
also has stable rank one.

Case 2.
A
has no nonzero projections.
By Theorem
7.11
, the canonical map
\Gamma

is surjective. Choose
e\in(A\otimes{\cal K})_{+}
with
0\leq e\leq 1

such that
\widehat{[e]}
is continuous on
\widetilde{QT}(A).

By Theorem
2.19
,
C=\mathrm{Her}(e)
has continuous scale.
By Brown’s stable isomorphism theorem (
[
4
]
),

C\otimes{\cal K}\cong A\otimes{\cal K}.
Therefore it suffices to show that
C
has
stable rank one (see
[
39
, Theorem 6.4]
). Hence, without loss of generality, we may assume that
A
has continuous scale (and
QT(A)\not=\emptyset
).

Let
A_{1}
be a
\sigma
-unital hereditary
C^{*}
-subalgebra of
A.

Let
a\in A_{1}^{\bf 1}
and let
\varepsilon>0.

Let
a_{1}:=a^{*}a+aa^{*}.

Then there is
\delta>0
such that

{{\|a-f_{\delta}({{a_{1}}})af_{\delta}({{a_{1}}})\|<\varepsilon/2.}}

Let
\bar{a}:=f_{\delta}({{a_{1}}})af_{\delta}({{a_{1}}}).

Since
A_{1}
has no nonzero projections,
we may assume that
[0,\delta]\subset{\rm sp}({{a_{1}}}).

Let
g\in C_{0}((0,1])_{+}
with
{\rm supp}(g)\subset[\delta/4,\delta/2],

then

{{b{{:}}=g({{a_{1}}})\neq 0\,\,\,{\rm and}\,\,\,b\bar{a}=\bar{a}b=0.}}

Let us consider

A_{2}=\mathrm{Her}_{A_{1}}(f_{\delta/8}({{a_{1}}})).

Note that
A_{2}
is simple,
A_{2}={\rm Ped}(A_{2})
and
QT(A_{2})\not=\emptyset

and has strict comparison.
Moreover, by Proposition
5.4
,
A_{2}
has T-tracial approximate oscillation zero.
Hence, by Theorem
6.6
,

l^{\infty}(A_{2})/I_{{}_{{\overline{QT(A_{2})}^{w}}}}
has stable rank one.
Note that
\bar{a},b\in A_{2}.

Note also that, since
A
is non-elementary,

\mathrm{Her}_{A_{2}}(b)
is infinite dimensional. It follows that there are
b_{1},b_{2}\in{{\mathrm{Her}_{A_{2}}(b)_{+}\setminus\{0\}}}

such that
b_{1}\bot b_{2}.

Since
\bar{a}^{*}\bar{a}+\bar{a}\bar{a}^{*},b_{1},b_{2}
are mutually orthogonal,
applying Lemma
9.3
,
we get two nilpotents
x,y\in A_{2}\subset A_{1}

such that
\|\bar{a}-xy\|<\varepsilon/2.

It follows that
\|a-xy\|<\varepsilon.

Therefore, for any
\sigma
-unital hereditary

C^{*}
-subalgebra
A_{1}\subset A,

any
a\in A_{1},

any
\varepsilon>0,

there are nilpotents
x,y\in A_{1}

such that
\|a-xy\|<\varepsilon.

Together with the facts that
A

is projectionless and assumed to have continuous scale,
applying
[
14
, Theorem 6.4]
, we
conclude that
A
has stable rank one.
∎

The proof of Theorem
1.1

Proof.

For (1)
\Rightarrow
(2), applying Theorem
7.11
, we know that
\Gamma
is surjective.
Then (2) follows from
Theorem
9.4
.

Both (2)
\Rightarrow
(3) and (2)
\Rightarrow
(4) are obvious.
That (3)
\Rightarrow
(2) follows from
[
1
]
.

For (4)
\Rightarrow
(1), we apply Theorem
5.10
.
That (1)
\Leftrightarrow
(5) follows from Theorem

8.5
and Theorem
8.11
. This ends the proof of Theorem
1.1
.
∎

Note that the separability condition is only used in the implication of (3)
\Rightarrow
(2).

We learned that the following is also obtained by S. Geffen and W. Winter.

Corollary 9.5
.

Let
A
be a
\sigma
-unital stably finite simple
C^{*}
-algebra  of real rank zero which has strict comparison.
Then
\Gamma
is surjective and
A
has stable rank one.

Proof.

Let
p\in A
be a non-zero projection and
B=pAp.
It suffices to show the statement holds for
B.

Note that
B
is unital and stably finite. By the paragraph right after the proof
of Theorem 3.3 of
[
3
]
,
B
has a 2-quasitrace (see also III. 1.3 of
[
2
]
).
By Proposition
5.8
,
A
has tracial approximate oscillation zero.
Then the corollary follows from Theorem
9.4
.
∎

Let
A
be a separable simple
C^{*}
-algebra  with
{\widetilde{QT}}(A)\setminus\{0\}\not=\emptyset.

Let
e\in{\rm Ped}(A)_{+}\setminus\{0\}.

Recall that
T_{e}=\{\tau\in{\widetilde{QT}}(A):\tau(e)=1\}

is a compact convex set
which is also a basis for the cone
\widetilde{QT}(A).

Corollary 9.6
.

Let
A
be a
\sigma
-unital
simple
C^{*}
-algebra
with
QT(A)\not=\emptyset
which has strict comparison.
Suppose that, for some
e\in{\rm Ped}(A)_{+}^{\bf 1}\setminus\{0\}

and
\partial_{e}(T_{e})

has countably many points.
Then
\Gamma
is surjective,
A
has stable rank one,
property (TM) and
T-tracial approximate oscillation zero.

Proof.

It follows from Theorem
5.9
that
A
has norm approximate oscillation zero.
Thus corollary follows from Theorem
9.4
immediately.
∎

The following is perhaps known but we are not able to locate it in the literature.

Proposition 9.7
.

Let
A
be a separable
C^{*}
-algebra  which has local finite nuclear dimension.
Then every hereditary
C^{*}
-subalgebra
B\subset A
also has local finite nuclear dimension.

Proof.

Let
B
be a hereditary
C^{*}
-subalgebra  of
A
. Let
\varepsilon>0
and
{\cal F}\subset B
be a
finite subset. To simplify notation, without loss of generality, we may assume that
{\cal F}\subset B^{\bf 1}
and
there is
e_{B}\in B_{+}^{\bf 1}

such that
e_{B}x=xe_{B}=x
for all
x\in{\cal F}.

Choose
\delta>0
as in Lemma 3.3 of
[
10
]
associated with
\varepsilon/4
(in place
of
\varepsilon
) and
\sigma=\varepsilon/4.
We may assume that
\delta<\varepsilon/4.

Since
A
has local finite nuclear dimension, there is a
C^{*}
-subalgebra
C\subset A
with
finite nuclear dimension, say
k
(
k\in\mathbb{N}{{\cup\{0\}}}
), such that

\displaystyle x\in_{\delta/2}C\,\,\,{\rm for\,\,\,all}\,\,\,x\in{\cal F}\cup\{e_{B}\}.

(e 9.33)

Choose
d\in C_{+}^{\bf 1}
such that
\|e_{B}-d\|<\delta.

Then, by Lemma 3.3 of
[
10
]
, there is a partial isometry
w\in A^{**}
such
that

\displaystyle ww^{*}f_{\varepsilon/4}(d)=f_{\varepsilon/4}(d)ww^{*}=f_{\varepsilon/4}(d),\,\,w^{*}cw\in\mathrm{Her}(e_{B})\subset B\,\,\,{\rm and}\,\,\,

(e 9.34)

\displaystyle\|w^{*}cw-c\|<(\varepsilon/4)\|c\|\,\,\,{\rm for\,\,\,all}\,\,\,c\in\overline{f_{\varepsilon/4}(d)Af_{\varepsilon/4}(d)}.

(e 9.35)

Set
C_{1}=w^{*}\overline{f_{\varepsilon/4}(d)Cf_{\varepsilon/4}(d)}w\subset B.

By Proposition 2.5 of
[
51
]
,
\overline{f_{\varepsilon/4}(d)Cf_{\varepsilon/4}(d)}
has nuclear dimension
k.

Since
C_{1}\cong\overline{f_{\varepsilon/4}(d)Cf_{\varepsilon/4}(d)},

C_{1}
has nuclear dimension
k.

We then estimate that

\displaystyle x\in_{\varepsilon}C_{1}\,\,\,{\rm for\,\,\,all}\,\,\,x\in{\cal F}.

(e 9.36)

Thus
B
has local finite nuclear dimension.
∎

As in
[
45
]
, we have the following
(note that, by
[
17
]
, since
A
is exact,
\widetilde{T}(A)=\widetilde{QT}(A)
):

Corollary 9.8
.

Let
A
be a separable exact simple
C^{*}
-algebra  with
{\widetilde{T}}(A){{\setminus\{0\}}}\not=\emptyset.

Suppose that
A
has strict comparison, T-tracial approximate oscillation zero
and has local finite nuclear dimension. Then
A\otimes{\cal Z}\cong A.

Proof.

Choose
e\in{\rm Ped}(A)_{+}^{\bf 1}\setminus\{0\}
and

B=\mathrm{Her}(e),
Then
{\rm Ped}(B)=B.
It suffices to show (see Cor. 3.1 of
[
48
]
)
that
B
is
{\cal Z}
-stable. Note that
B
has strict comparison, and by Proposition
9.7
, has local finite nuclear dimension. Since
B
also has T-tracial approximate oscillation zero
(see
5.4
), by Theorem
7.11
,
\Gamma
is surjective.
It follows that
B
has
m
-almost divisibility
for some
m
(in fact
m
can be zero).
By

[
46
, Theorem 8.5 (iii)]
,
B
is
{\cal Z}
-stable.
∎

Remark 9.9
.

At least in the unital case, the condition that
A
has local finite nuclear dimension
in Corollary
9.8
can be further weakened
to that
A
is amenable and has weak tracial finite nuclear dimension (see Definition 8.1 and Theorem 8.3 of
[
28
]
).

Remark 9.10
.

(1) Note that, in Theorem
1.1
and Corollary
9.6
,
we do not assume that
A
is amenable or even exact.

(2) Usually, the condition that
A
has strict comparison implies that
A
has at least one
densely defined non-zero
2-quasitrace. However, one may insist that the condition that
A
has strict comparison
means that, if
A
has no non-zero 2-quasitraces,
A
is purely infinite. In that case the assumption
in
Theorem
1.1
(part of the assumption of
Corollary
9.8
) may be replaced by that
A
is finite and has strict comparison.

(3) On the other hand, if one assumes that
\mathrm{Cu}(A)
is almost unperforated and
A
is not purely infinite,
then, by
[
44
]
,
A
has strict comparison (in the usual sense) (see also Remark 2.5 and Proposition 4.9
of
[
16
]
). Conversely, if
A
has strict comparison (in usual sense),

\mathrm{Cu}(A)
is almost unperforated. Therefore, if one prefers not to mention
2-quasitraces in Theorem
1.1
, one could use the condition that
A
is finite and
\mathrm{Cu}(A)
is almost unperforated.

(4) If
A
is a unital stably finite simple
C^{*}
-algebra, then,
by
[
43
, Theorem 6.1]
(see also

[
8
, Corollary 4.7]
and

[
2
, Theorem II.2.2]
),

A
has at least one non-trivial 2-quasitrace. So, in the unital case, we may assume that
A
is stably finite instead
assume that
A
has a non-trivial 2-quasitrace. This also works for the case that
A
is not unital
but
K_{0}(A)_{+}\not=\{0\}.
However, when
A
is stably projectionless, the situation is somewhat different.
Nevertheless, we may proceed this as follows:

We assume that
A
is a separable simple
C^{*}
-algebra.
Recall that an element
a\in{\rm Ped}(A)_{+}
is infinite,
if there are nonzero elements
{{b,\ }}c\in{\rm Ped}(A)_{+}
such that
bc=0,

b+c\lesssim c
and
c\lesssim a.

A
is said to be finite, if there are no infinite elements in
{\rm Ped}(A)_{+}.

A
is said to be
stably finite, if
M_{n}(A)
is finite for each
n
(see Definition 1.1 of
[
30
]
and Definition 4.7 of
[
16
]
, for example).

Choose
e\in{\rm Ped}(A)_{+}\setminus\{0\}
and consider
B=\mathrm{Her}(e).

Without loss of generality, we may well assume that
A=B
for convenience.
Define
K_{0}^{*}(A)
(using
W(A)
not
\mathrm{Cu}(A)
) exactly the same way as in section 4 of

[
8
]
. Note that Lemma 4.1 of
[
8
]
holds automatically with the definition above.
The same definition of order there (before Proposition 4.2 of
[
8
]
) also works in this case.
In other words,
so defined
K_{0}^{*}(A)
is a (directed) ordered group and the stably finiteness
ensures that
K_{0}^{*}(A)
is not zero. Since
A
is simple, Proposition 4.2 of
[
8
]

still holds. We now return to the paragraph right after the proof Theorem 3.3 of
[
3
]
.
Note that
(K_{0}^{*}(A),K_{0}^{*}(A)_{+},[e])
is a scaled ordered group which has a state, and which
gives a dimension function. By II. 2.2 of
[
2
]
, the dimension function just mentioned gives a 2-quasitrace on
A.

Therefore, we may replace the condition that
{\widetilde{QT}}(A)\setminus\{0\}\not=\emptyset
by
the condition that
A
is stably finite (recall that we assume that
A
is simple) in Theorem
1.1
(see also
[
19
]

for the case that
A
is exact).

(5) One may notice that the condition that
A
has strict comparison and
\Gamma
is surjective
implies that there is an isomorphism
\Gamma^{\sim}:\mathrm{Cu}(A)\to V(A)\sqcup({\rm LAff}_{+}(\widetilde{QT}(A))\setminus\{0\})

(see also
2.13
).
